Appendix A

Glossary of Terms
Actions: An approach or method for attaining objectives and resolving specific issues. Generally start with
a verb.

Baseline: A starting point, known measure, or position that can be used for future comparisons.

Clustered subdivision/Cluster development: The grouping of residential properties on lots smaller than
allowed in the underlying zone to reduce infrastructure costs and environmental impacts and to reserve
otherwise developable land for open space or recreation.

CodeRED program: Opt-in telephone, email, text, and social media emergency warning system (e.g.
evacuation notices, utility outages, fire, floods), available to Canyon residents and to visitors via the
CodeRED app.

Dark Sky: Programs affiliated with the International Dark-Sky Association (IDA), who works to protect the
night skies for present and future generations.

Digital infrastructure: Digital infrastructure comprises the physical resources that are needed to enable
the use of data, computerized devices, methods, systems, and processes.

Economic development: Meaningful participation in economic, social, political, and cultural process and
institutions so that people can improve their conditions; inclusive of considerations such as upward
mobility, quality of life, diversity of businesses, and the ability for residents to meet daily employment,
shopping, service, and financing needs.

Ecosystem-based approach: A holistic approach to environmental planning which aims to ensure the
protection, maintenance, and restoration of ecological health and biodiversity at a range of spatial and
temporal scales and recognizes that human-environmental relationships are an inextricable component
of the system.

Firewise: A voluntary program that provides a framework to help neighbors get organized, find direction,
and take action to increase the ignition resistance of their homes and community.

Goals: Broad qualitative statements about what the organization hopes to achieve by following the
General Plan.

Growth: Any increase in a measure over time.

Linguistic isolation: A term used by the US Census Bureau to describe limited English-speaking
households.

Metric: A system or standard of measurement or evaluation that can be used to assess progress toward
meeting goals.

National Emergency Alert System (EAS): National public warning system that requires radio and TV
broadcasters, cable TV, wireless cable systems, satellite and wireline operators to provide the President
with capability to address the American people within 10 minutes during a national emergency.

Objectives: Statements regarding how improvements will take place and therefore tend to be quantitative
in nature. Usually begin with something akin to "increase" or "decrease."



                                                                                                            1
Policies: Statements that inform elected officials and decision-makers. Should provide a path towards
achieving goals.

Programs: Intentional, structured and supported efforts to progress the attainment of goals.

Peri-urban: Qualities, or the distinctive aspects of planning associated with a relatively rural area located
immediately adjacent to a city or urban area.

Precautionary principle: An approach to making environmental decisions that prioritizes: taking
preventive action in the face of uncertainty; shifting the burden of proof to the proponents of an activity;
exploring a wide range of alternatives to possibly harmful actions; and increasing public participation in
decision making.

Infrastructure: The set of fundamental structures and facilities serving a society and enabling its
operation.
Resilience: The ability for complex systems to change, adapt, and transform, in positive ways and within
a reasonable timeframe, following hardships and disasters.
Social cohesion: The extent to which a society or community works to ensure the well-being of all its
members, fights exclusion and marginalization, creates a sense of belonging, promotes trust, and offers
its members the opportunity of upward social mobility.

Social infrastructure: The assets that act as a conduit to bring people together and strengthen
relationships; includes schools, community groups, outdoor markets, coffee shops, libraries, parks,
religious institutions, and other physical assets that contribute to a culture of neighborliness,
acceptance, and connection.

Sustainability: The ability to exist constantly; refers generally to the capacity for the biosphere and
human civilization to co-exist in a balanced state.

Sustainable development: Development that meets the needs of the present without compromising the
ability of future generations to meet their own needs.

Systems: Complex interconnected structures or networks; can include social and ecological
interconnections that account for the relationships of people within a community to each other as well as
the interactions of animals, plants, and climate with humans and communities.

Targets: Numerically-defined thresholds that objectives are designed to meet.

Unreinforced masonry: Structures that do not have modern safety features, such as steel-
reinforcements, roof to wall attachments, and wall to floor attachments.

Values: Beliefs about desirable states or modes of conduct that guide behavior and prioritization of goals
and objectives.

Vision: A description of the “preferred future”; a statement that describes how the future will look if the
issues that are important to the community are perfectly addressed.




                                                                                                           2
Wildland-Urban Interface (WUI): A transitional area between open space/wildlands and human
development. Communities in the WUI are more at risk of natural hazards such as wildfires and can be
disruptive to sensitive ecological features.

                                Transportation Planning Principles Workshop Report
                                             Thursday January 30, 2020 6:30-8:30pm
                                 2001 South State Street, South Building, S1-120 and South Atrium

        PARTICIPANTS
        32 members of Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District (MSD) Planning Commissions, Councils,
        General Plan Steering Committees, and some members of the public who are residents of MSD areas.

        STAFF AND SPEAKERS
        MSD Staff: Lupita McClenning, Wendy Gurr, Mikala Jordan, Max Johnson, Daniel Quintanilla, Alex
        Rudowski, Travis Hair, Kate Davies

        Speakers: Ted Knowlton, Deputy Director, Wasatch Front Regional Council; Jon Larsen, Director, Division
        of Transportation Planning, Salt Lake City

        Other staff: Three Salt Lake County staff members also sat in on presentations and some of the
        discussions but did not participate in the activities.

        OBJECTIVES
           • To provide background information on transportation trends and innovations to key MSD
              decision makers;
           • To produce a suite of key transportation principles that can be used to guide transportation
              planning across MSD; and
           • To produce a suite of key transportation principles that can be used to link MSD to surrounding
              areas.

        BACKGROUND AND SCOPE
        At this workshop we started to develop a suite of transportation planning values and principles that can
        sit across the entire jurisdiction of the MSD, with the understanding that these are only a general set of
        guidelines and may be prioritized and implemented differently in each Metro Township or Area based on
        future local open houses, surveys, and other types of engagement with the communities of these areas.

        Definitions:
        Values are beliefs about desirable states or modes of conduct that guide behaviour and prioritization.

        Principles clarify the overarching values that are to be used and evaluated for all decisions, thus forming
        the foundation for implementing strategies that require a unified management approach.

        Values and principles will ultimately be linked to outcomes and performance indicators (see example,
        Table 1). This process will help to ensure that planning decisions are made based on the values held by
        MSD communities.

EXPERT PRESENTATIONS
Ted Knowlton, Deputy Director, Wasatch Front Regional Council

Ted encouraged workshop participants to think big - What are your Metro Townships’ overall issues and
how does transportation help address them?

He outlined some current trends in transportation planning, particularly the decline in investments in
suburban living, and the rising interest and investment in features such as walkability and a short
commute (often as part of a commitment to improving human and environmental health).

He outlined how mapping out “Access to Opportunities” can help re-think transportation planning, linking
it more specifically to housing and economic development plans. See for example:
https://wfrc.org/wasatch-choice-map/#currentTabIndex=2&scale=144448&sideBarClosed=false&x=-
12463593&y=4961808

Ted also outlined some of the specific features that residents are usually looking for in terms of walk- and
bike-ability, emphasizing that small improvements can often make a big difference.

Finally, Ted explained how these new ways of planning for and implementing transportation options are
being supported across Utah. He pointed in particular to UDOT’s new approach to programming
investments, which aligns with a set of core values (see Figure 1).




Figure 1: Core values outlined in the most recent draft UDOT transportation vision
https://uvision.utah.gov/

            Planning & Development Services • 2001 South State Street, N3-600 • Salt Lake City, Utah 84190
                                         https://msd.utah.gov/msd-home/pds/

                                                                                                                       5
                                                                                                             Page 3 of 8




Jon Larsen, Director, Division of Transportation Planning, Salt Lake City

Jon discussed some of the transportation innovations that could change the way that we move around
future urban environments. He emphasized that change is the only certainty!

In order to deal with change, he suggested relying on two key principles:

    1) Timeless investments are the stable foundation of the system.
    2) Flexibility provides opportunity for innovation and growth.

He encouraged planning for “the original mode” (walking), which never goes out of style, pointing out
that “People-First” streets age well. He encouraged everyone to consider how streets function (street
typologies) to do much more than just move vehicles – they also move people, they can help make a
place unique and vibrant, they can enhance the livability and sustainability of an area, and they can
provide access to a variety of services and service providers. Transportation planning should develop
thoughtful designs that balance those needs and match them with the surrounding context.

He finished by describing some of the many street design options that are out there which could be
embraced and reimagined for MSD communities (see for examples Street Design by Dover &
Massengale).


BREAKOUT SESSION 1: VALUES
Participants counted off into five small groups and were assigned to a facilitator who would guide them
through two short activities (Breakout Session 1: Values, and Breakout Session 2: Principles). Participants
were given an initial list of potential transportation values to consider (Appendix A). These could be added
to or otherwise adapted as needed with the breakout group. Participants were asked to consider which
three to five values on the list were MOST important in terms of planning for future transportation across
the MSD. After describing why their top three values were most important to them and listening to
others in their group, participants could change their initial votes to come up with a final group tally of
their top transportation values.

These top transportation values were collected from all five groups and combined into a total tally for the
whole group. The top transportation values to emerge from this process (those that received eight or
more votes across all groups) are shown in Figure 2.

BREAKOUT SESSION 2: PRINCIPLES
Five of the top values that emerged from Breakout Session 1 were then given back to the groups (one
value per group) to discuss some principles that might help to clarify the values and how transportation
plans could be implemented in alignment with those values (Figure 3). Some ideas regarding principles
that emerged from these discussions are outlined below:

Group A: Clean air
   • Develop programs that support emissions reductions (e.g. increased transit and micro mobility
       options, consider a range of ways to reduce trips)
   • Provide resources for education and outreach (e.g. implement an effective campaign for
       carpooling and other clean air transportation options)
   • Develop an app for schools – support carpooling or walking as a group

Group B: Safe, user friendly streets for all types of users
   • Encourage modes of travel that don’t negatively impact on air quality
   • Consider ages and stages of mode users (child-elderly)


             Planning & Development Services • 2001 South State Street, N3-600 • Salt Lake City, Utah 84190
                                          https://msd.utah.gov/msd-home/pds/

                                                                                                                             7
                                                                                                            Page 5 of 8




    •   All actions and projects are prioritized to improve safety and mode choice
    •   Respect the beauty and sustainability of the environment
    •   Wherever possible, physically separate different traffic streams
    •   All users respect the roads and trails
    •   Where possible, use natural means of traffic calming
    •   Sidewalk inventory
    •   How our streets could incorporate movement

Group C: Aligning local and regional needs
   • Be aware of existing authority (e.g. UT is a property rights state)
   • Need to educate about issues that cross boundaries
   • Need to have a strong vision - what your community should be and the ability to protect this
   • Local economies need to be connected to this vision (what do you want and why)
   • Ordinances need to reflect values, but also be flexible and provide options
   • Sidewalk infrastructure connectivity – improve economies of scale, connect people to amenities
   • Evaluation and monitoring – crucial to include this from the beginning

Group D: Ongoing engagement of stakeholders in local decision making
   • Outreach needed – use multiple ways to connect with a range of audiences, conduct a baseline
       survey, try to connect with people when they are out having fun
   • Recognize and appreciate diversity – plan for ESOL, different ways and venues to connect
   • Keep it positive (overall) so that people stay engaged
   • Outcome-focused efforts needed so that people can see progress
   • Public spaces should be managed by the public – to build a sense of ownership and
       connectedness, can have services days etc.
   • Have mores MSD-wide events! The cooperative nature is awesome, helps to build a collective
       identity and sense of purpose

Group E: Quality transportation choices
    • Multi-modal choices
    • Stronger and more robust Trax infrastructure
    • Incentives for clean transportation solutions
            o Electric, trails, walkable neighbourhoods
    • Safe user-friendly streets for all users – design is key
    • Choices should evoke a sense of place
    • Carefully consider how to design streets for traffic capacity e.g.
            o Widen streets and increase speeds
            o Control for street width and decrease speeds

Environmental

Clean water
Sustainability
Preservation of open space
Natural resource conservation/restoration
Reduction of light pollution
Resilience to climate change
Responsible use of materials
Good nutrient management practices
Increase energy efficiency
Mitigate emissions (e.g. GHG)
Good health and clean air

Social & Cultural
Good health (of individuals and communities, mental and physical)
Connected communities
Safe, user friendly streets for all types of users
Good access to key destinations/services
Good access to open space and recreational opportunities
Preserve/revive high priority sites (e.g. historic structures, focus on growth and redevelopment for infill)
Quality transportation choices (e.g. moving people, not just cars)
Manageable and reliable traffic conditions
Point to point access for micro-mobility and shared vehicles
Multimodal infrastructure (both the travel lanes and the on-street parking)
Utilization of digital infrastructure
Adaptive, flexible components
Growth management (e.g. establish measures to control growth/sprawl)

Economic
Strong economy
Access to economic and educational opportunities
Affordable living expenses (including transportation costs)
Support housing options
Fiscally responsible communities and infrastructure
Link economic development with transportation and housing decisions
Low carbon economy

Process
Ongoing engagement of stakeholders in local decision making
Commit to an outcomes-based approach to planning, design, and engineering
Environmental justice
Equitable, inclusive, and inviting process
Aligning local and regional needs

BACKGROUND AND SCOPE
This report summarizes the results of an online Transportation and Land Use survey that was sent by
email to local leaders across the MSD member communities (e.g. Councilors, Planning Commissioners,
General Plan Steering Committees) and posted on social media accounts and associated websites in April
and May 2020. Between 4/9/20 and 5/14/20, 464 responses to the survey were received. For a complete
copy of the survey questions, see Appendix A.

The survey was intended for community members living in the MSD service area (e.g. Brighton,
Copperton, Emigration Canyon, Kearns, Magna, White City, or Unincorporated Salt Lake County). It was
expected that the survey would take approximately five to ten minutes to complete. Respondents were
informed that the information that they submitted would be used to guide the development of general
plans for their area. They were also informed that their responses would be confidential and could not be
linked back to them in any way. Once their responses were submitted, they could not be changed.
Respondents were encouraged to answer the questions as honestly as possible.


Study objectives
    •   To gather information on current and future transportation and land use preferences that will be
        used to guide the development of general plans for communities that are members of the MSD
        service area.
    •   To support fact-based decision making that will maximize the health, safety, and economic well-
        being of all people living in an MSD service area community.

Respondents
    •   Overall, respondents were relatively evenly divided between male and female, with 48% male,
        49% female, and 3% who preferred not to say.

    •   Respondents were also relatively well divided across age ranges:
        18-35: 10%
        35-49: 33%
        50-64: 33%
        65+: 19%
        Prefer not to say: 5%

    •   All but four of the respondents said that they hold a valid driver’s license, and all but seven said
        that they own a personal vehicle.

                                                                                               Page 6 of 59




SUMMARY OF FINDINGS ACROSS ALL MSD STUDY AREAS

Where do you live?




Figure 1: Responses to question 1: Where do you live?


How often do you use the following modes of transportation?
In response to question 2. How often do you use the following modes of transportation? 65% of all
respondents said that they drive alone daily. A much smaller proportion of respondents, 17%, said that
they use alternative modes of transportation such as carpool, bus, train or bike on a daily basis.


How often do you link more than one destination/mode when you travel (trip chaining)?
23% of respondents said that they link more than one destination and/or mode of transportation daily
(trip chaining).


On a scale of very important, important, or not important; which of the following
transportation problems are the most important to address?
The transportation concerns that were cited by respondents as “very important” are listed in Table 1.

                                                                                                     74
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


1.0 Executive Summary
1.1 Population
In 2018, the population of Emigration Canyon was estimated at 1,828. The population of the Canyon has
been increasing since 2000 at a rate over twice that of Salt Lake County. The Township represents an older
population, with a median age of 48.1. The population is expected to continue ageing, with 24.2 percent
of residents expected to be over the age of 65 by the year 2024. During that same time period, the
population of children under the age of 18 is expected to increase to 22.3 percent. Emigration Canyon
Metro Township is a highly educated and wealthy population. Over 92 percent of adults age 25 and over
have had at least some college training, while 45.2 percent of those adults hold a graduate or professional
degree. The median household income for the Canyon is $134,821, nearly twice the median income of
Salt Lake County. Household size has been decreasing in the Township, with the average household size
estimated at 2.68 in the year 2018. The population of Emigration Canyon is predominantly White, Non-
Hispanic. In upcoming years, the percentage of residents reporting that they belong to a minority race or
ethnicity is expected to increase, but not at the rate of increase expected in Salt Lake County at large.

1.2 Economy
There were 962 employed civilians residing in Emigration Canyon as of 2018, for a labor force participation
rate of 66.9 percent. Unemployment increased in the Canyon between 2010 and 2018, despite decreases
at the County, State, and National levels. The 2018 unemployment rate was approximately 4.2 percent.
The poverty rate of the Township was 13.1 percent in 2018 – lower than Salt Lake City’s rate but higher
than the County’s. ‘Arts, entertainment, & recreation, accommodation & food services’ is the largest
industry by employment, employing 38 percent of the Canyon’s civilian labor force. Most workers
commute out of the Canyon, with 95.1 percent employed somewhere in Salt Lake County. Salt Lake City
and Millcreek employ the largest number of residents from Emigration Canyon. 76.3 percent of the civilian
employed population drives alone to work, although the percentage of the population walking and biking
to work or working from home has increased in recent years.

1.3 Housing
Between 2010 and 2018, the housing unit vacancy rate in Emigration Canyon fell drastically from 20
percent to 9 percent. Increases in occupancy occurred primarily for owner-occupied units, with renter-
occupied units comprising only 9 percent of the total housing stock in 2018. The vast majority (99 percent)
of Emigration Canyon’s housing units are described as single-family-detached, and 41.6 percent of
households are comprised of three or more persons. The condition of housing units in the Canyon is good
overall, with no units reporting lack of plumbing, kitchen facilities, or telephone service. However, the
housing stock is old and expected to continue aging. The percentage of housing units built prior to 1980
increased from 37.8 percent to 51.4 percent between 2010 and 2018. The median rent in 2018 was $1,224
(an increase from 2010), while the median monthly cost for owners was $2,488 in 2018, a decrease from
the 2010 cost. Given the expense of housing, many Emigration Canyon households are cost-burdened.
Every household making less than $35,000 in the Canyon spends over 30 percent of its income on housing
costs. Even among the highest income bracket ($75,000+), 2.8 percent of households are cost-burdened
and 15.7 percent are at risk of becoming cost-burdened. These numbers will continue to worsen as

                 Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 4

                                                                                                        75
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


housing costs rise faster than incomes. Residents of Emigration Canyon are quite rooted in their
community, with 70.6 percent of households reporting having lived in their home for over ten years.

Rising housing costs and growth pressures from Salt Lake Valley will continue to challenge Emigration
Canyon into the next decade and beyond. Informed, data-driven decision-making will help the community
prepare for upcoming changes and preserve the aspects of Canyon life that they hold dear.


2.0 Introduction
2.1 Using this Document
This technical assessment provides an overview of sociodemographic trends and external conditions
influencing Emigration Canyon Metro Township (ECMT). The assessment is intended to provide planners,
residents and other stakeholders with the data and context needed to make informed decisions regarding
planning, policy, and community development for ECMT. The data provided also sets the groundwork for
a general plan which will influence the development of the canyon for years to come. This document
includes an introduction to the setting and history of Emigration Canyon, an overview of population trends
and demographics, an analysis of economic conditions in the Township, data on ECMT’s housing stock,
and a summary of existing public infrastructure and facilities. This document presents robust data for
consideration, but how the data is used will ultimately be the decision of the community of Emigration
Canyon Metro Township.

2.2 Methodology
The Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District (MSD), the organization responsible for providing
planning services to ECMT, is committed to providing its member communities with quality data. Data for
this report was primarily gathered from the U.S. Census Bureau’s American Community Survey (“ACS”)
Reports and Esri Business Analyst’s detailed community profiles. The most recent data available was for
the year 2018, except for some projections used throughout the document. Where projections are shown,
the source of the data and/or methodology used to make the projection are provided along with the
numbers. While Census data does not always perfectly capture the true population statistics for smaller
areas such as ECMT, it is the most complete and accurate dataset available. The Census data in this report
comes from ACS 5-Year Estimates (or decennial census numbers where available), which aggregate data
over a five-year period to provide the best estimate of current conditions.

The Emigration Canyon Census-Designated Place (“CDP”) is the geographic entity for which numbers were
gathered. Data for Emigration Canyon Metro Township is compared to that for Salt Lake County to give
the reader a better understanding of the greater context of the area’s sociodemographic trends.
Comparisons are also made to Salt Lake City since it is the municipality immediately adjacent to ECMT and
the one most likely to influence growth and development of the area.




                Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 5

                                                                                                       76
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


2.3 Emigration Canyon Metro Township
Emigration Canyon Metro Township comprises approximately nineteen square miles of land in Utah’s
Wasatch Mountain Range. The Township shares a border with Salt Lake City to the west. Emigration
Canyon Road is the only arterial road in the township. It runs approximately 7.8 miles from Salt Lake City’s
Hogle Zoo to UT-65, which also connects to US-80 (See Figure 1). Emigration Canyon is home to
approximately 1,828 residents (ACS 5-Year Estimates, 2018) and serves as an important recreational
destination for bikers, hikers, and wildlife viewers from neighboring municipalities in the Salt Lake Valley.

Figure 1: Emigration Canyon Boundaries and Points of Interest




Source: Prepared by Greater Salt Lake MSD GIS



                 Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 6

                                                                                                          77
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


2.4 History of the Canyon
The rich history of Emigration Canyon has strongly influenced the present community. Even before
Mormon pioneers trekked through Emigration Canyon on their way to settle the Salt Lake Valley, native
peoples were relying on the Canyon’s water supply and abundant wildlife for sustenance. Although there
is little evidence that these early populations lived in Emigration Canyon, it is believed by researchers that
both Ute and Shoshone tribes hunted the Canyon’s forest and fished the Emigration Creek, once heavily
populated with trout (Carlstrom & Furse, 2019). Residents have even discovered arrowheads buried on
their properties (ibid).

Although first discovered and cultivated by early native tribes, Emigration Canyon derives its name from
the many pioneers who passed through it as they ‘emigrated’ to the Salt Lake Valley and beyond. The
Donner-Reed party was one of the first and most notable early groups of emigrants, traveling the canyon
in 1846, as a part of their ill-fated journey on the Hasting’s Cutoff (Carlstrom & Furse, 2019). And in July
of 1847, Mormon refugees from the east made their final camp in Emigration Canyon before descending
into the Salt Lake Valley, the place that they would ultimately stay and make their home (ibid). As they
established their new civilization in the valley, the Canyon became a path for future travelers, mail, and
freight, as well as the source of timber and stone for building.

Over time, several businesses established themselves in Emigration Canyon, including the Wagener
Brewery (1864-1914), Pinecrest Inn (1913-1949), and the famous Ruth’s Diner (1949-Present). These
businesses took advantage of Salt Lake residents craving an escape into the cooler temperatures of the
Canyon, as well as the more lenient laws that came because of their location outside of City boundaries
(Carlstrom & Furse, 2019). From 1907 to 1917, the Emigration Canyon Railroad operated between Salt
Lake City and quarries at the top of the Canyon. Although the original purpose of the rail line was to carry
heavy stone from the mountain quarries down into the City for building, the advent of Portland Cement
soon made the stone industry obsolete, and the railroad began to primarily serve passengers (ibid).
Residents of Salt Lake Valley could take the train from the City up into the alpine territories of the Canyon
to enjoy fishing, hiking, and picnicking. Eventually, the presence of the train led to further development
of the Canyon. Streetcar suburbs popped up near train stops, and the Pinecrest Inn (see Figure 2) was
built near the end of the line to accommodate guests with overnight lodging, food, and entertainment
(ibid). Although the Emigration Canyon Railroad was financially unsustainable and dismantled after just
ten years, the Canyon continued to thrive. The land that had been cleared for the railroad tracks provided
automobile access, and residents from the surrounding valleys continued to establish summer cabins
throughout the Canyon’s wilderness.

Autonomy has long been a core value of the Emigration Canyon community. In 1950, the Emigration
Canyon Planning District formed to represent the many interests of residents, namely water quality, litter
pick-up, wilderness preservation, and maintenance of the Canyon’s low cost of living (Carlstrom & Furse,
2019). Very early on, the Planning District decided to implement a zoning structure which significantly
limited opportunities for commercial development in the Canyon - an attempt to preserve the quiet and
natural environment that had drawn residents to settle in the area. Attempts to expand development in
the canyon have consistently met with resistance from canyon residents over the years (see Carlstrom &

                 Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 7

                                                                                                           78
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


Furse, 2019 for several examples). Since then, the Canyon has continued to show ownership over its own
planning and decision-making, and several community interest groups (e.g. Emigration Canyon
Community Council, Emigration Canyon Sustainability Aliiance) have formed over the years to address
issues facing life in the Canyon (e.g. littering, water pollution, fire danger, vandalism, and growth
pressures). In 2017, Emigration Canyon was incorporated as a Metro Township and continues to be served
by a Metro Township Council and Planning Commission.

Today, Emigration Canyon is beloved both by residents and visitors. It is frequented by hikers and bikers,
looking for refuge in the vast wilderness surrounding the Salt Lake Valley. Although the environment has
been altered by humans, it is still possible to see coyote, mule deer, elk, rattlesnakes, fox, porcupine,
cougars, bobcats, moose, and several species of bird (Carlstrom & Furse, 2019). Formal and informal
recreational opportunities abound, including Camp Kostopulos, which has existed in Emigration Canyon
since 1971 and provides recreational, educational, and personal development opportunities for persons
of all abilities (Kostopulos Dream Foundation, 2019). Since 1961, Emigration Canyon has also been
recognized as a National Historic Landmark, symbolizing the uniqueness of this Canyon and its importance
to the surrounding Salt Lake Valley.

Figure 2: Old Postcard of Pinecrest Inn and the Emigration Canyon Railroad.




Source: Utah Historical Society.

                                                                                                       79

3.0 Population
3.1 Total Population
Emigration Canyon Metro Township occupies nineteen (19) square miles of land along Emigration Canyon
Road, east of Salt Lake City. In 2018, the population of Emigration Canyon was estimated at 1,828 (ACS 5-
Year Estimates, 2018). Table 1 compares population trends between Emigration Canyon, Salt Lake City,
the Salt Lake County and the State, from 2000 to 2018. Like the rest of the county and state, the population
of Emigration Canyon has grown significantly over the last two decades (Table 1).

Table 1: Total Population Comparison

Table 2 shows population projections for ECMT and Salt Lake County for the years 2020, 2030, 2040, and
2050. Projections for Salt Lake County come from the Kem C. Gardner Policy Institute’s County Projections.
Data for Emigration Canyon was calculated based on historic ratios of growth rates as detailed in the table
notes. Emigration Canyon is expected to grow at a faster pace than Salt Lake County as a whole, consistent
with trends from 2000 to 2018. The growth rate will likely slow down as 2050 draws nearer, but by then
the population may have increased to over 3,000 residents.

While these numbers are useful for informing future decision-making, it should be noted that they are
not absolute. Projections are modeled from current trends and cannot account for future planning and
policy decisions, economic changes, or environmental events that may alter the pace of growth.

3.2 Esri Tapestries
Esri Business Analyst produces tapestry segment analyses for various geographies across the United
States. These tapestry segments divide the population into groups based on sociodemographic status,
shopping patterns, and place of residence. Residents of Emigration Canyon are classified as ‘Top Tier’ by
Esri’s tapestry system.

The Top Tier tapestry segment is notable for its affluence (three times the U.S. median household income),
high educational attainment, and preference for socially responsible products (Esri Business Analyst
Tapestry Segmentation, 2020). The median age of the tapestry segment is 47.3 and management is the
most common occupation; 90.2 percent own their home (ibid). Households in the Top Tier tapestry
segment tend to spend lavishly on their homes, health, and wellbeing (including high-end spas and fitness
centers), and on vacations. But spending on education may be even more notable, with members of the
Top Tier tapestry segment spending 281 percent more than the national average on education (ibid).

Top Tier is the dominant tapestry segment in Emigration Canyon, but that does not mean that every
resident of the Canyon will fit the characteristics of the group. Tapestry segments are most useful for
determining the preferences of a group of people, especially when it comes to market spending and
lifestyle habits.

3.3 Age Characteristics
Table 3 shows the median age for Emigration Canyon Metro Township increased from 42.8 to 48.1
between 2010 and 2018. But the median age projected for 2024 is expected to decrease slightly. While
Salt Lake City, Salt Lake County, and the State of Utah represent young populations expected to age slowly
over the next few years, Emigration Canyon represents an older population which may experience an
influx of slightly younger residents in the future.

3.5 Racial Distribution
Emigration Canyon has traditionally had a very homogenous racial and ethnic profile. Between 2010 and
2018, there was very little change in the percentage of the population identifying as White Only (Table 8).
In 2024, there is expected to be a very modest increase in the percentage of minority racial and ethnic
populations, but that increase will be seen mostly among those identifying as Asian. The percentage of
persons identifying as Black/African American or ‘Some other Race’ is expected to decrease. Similarly, a
decrease in both the count and percentage of people identifying as Hispanic and/or Latino/a is expected
to occur for Emigration Canyon between 2018 and 2024.

3.6 Educational Attainment
Residents of Emigration Canyon Metro Township are highly educated. In 2018, nearly half (45.2 percent)
of residents aged 25 and over held a Graduate or Professional Degree, compared to just 12.6 percent for
Salt Lake County (Table 9). Less than 1 percent of Emigration Canyon’s population has not received a High
School Diploma. Between 2010 and 2018, educational attainment decreased slightly, with the percentage
of people with no college education increasing from 4.6 percent to 7.9 percent (Table 9). Even if this trend
continues, Emigration Canyon is expected to maintain a population with higher educational attainment
than Salt Lake County as a whole.

3.7 Income and Earnings
Table 10 shows changes in median household income between 2010 and 2018 for Emigration Canyon,
Salt Lake City, the County, and the State. While the reference geographies saw over 20 percent increases
in median household income, Emigration Canyon saw a 2 percent decline. Nonetheless, the median
household income for the Canyon far exceeds that of Salt Lake City, the County, and the State. The
Township’s 2018 median household income of $134,821 is nearly twice that of the State of Utah’s
($68,374).

Table 10: Median Household Income Comparison




Source: U.S. Census Bureau ACS 5-Year Estimates 2010 and 2018.


Figure 5 shows the 2010 and 2018 household income distributions for Emigration Canyon Metro
Township. In 2010, 48 percent of households had an income of $150,000 or greater (Figure 5). Nineteen
percent of households made less than $50,000, and no household had an income less than $10,000. From
2010 to 2018, there was some growth in middle income brackets. The percentage of households with an
income of $150,000 or greater dropped to 41 percent, and the percentage of households with an income
less than $50,000 increased to 20 percent. However, the largest increase was on the lowest end of the
spectrum, with households making less than $10,000 increasing from zero to ten percent. This is opposite
from the county trend which showed a decrease in the lowest income brackets and an increase in the
highest.

In both 2010 and 2018, the mean household income of Emigration Canyon was greater than twice that of
Salt Lake County. In both the Township and County, the percentage of households with earnings dropped
slightly between 2010 and 2018. As the percentage of households with traditional earnings decreased,
there was a subsequent increase in the percentage of households with retirement income – evidence of
an ageing population. The percentage of households receiving Social Security or other supplemental
security rose for Salt Lake County, while the same sources of income saw decreases in Emigration Canyon
(Table 12). In 2018, less than ten percent of Emigration Canyon households received Social Security
benefits. For those households that did receive Social Security, the mean amount was slightly higher than
the mean amount received by Salt Lake County households during the same year.

Median earnings by gender for 2010 to 2018 are described in Table 13. Emigration Canyon shows
interesting trends in earnings per gender during this time frame. While the earnings of full-time women
workers increased by thirty percent between 2010 and 2018, the earnings of full-time male workers
decreased over ten percent (Table 13). Women’s earnings during this time grew faster than in any other
referenced geography, but men’s earnings fell in Emigration Canyon (whereas they rose in every other
geography). The exact reason for this pattern is unknown. However, the population pyramid presented in
Figure 2 showed that the bulk of the male population in the Canyon was aged 60-64, as opposed to the
female population which was aged 50-54 (almost a decade younger). It is possible that between 2010 and
2018, a large group of the male population of the Township retired (removing their high earnings from
the aggregate data), while the slightly younger population of women continued working in senior
positions. Regardless, few municipalities can boast such high earnings for female employees. Emigration
Canyon represents one of the few places where a wage gap is not apparent between male and female
earners.

4.0 Economic Conditions
4.1 Labor Force
Table 14 shows civilian labor force classes for workers in Emigration Canyon, Salt Lake City, the County,
and the State in 2018. Emigration Canyon has 962 employed civilians, for a labor force participation rate
of 66.9 percent (ACS 5-Year Estimates 2018). Most of the employed population of Emigration Canyon is
comprised of private, for-profit wage and salary workers, but over 15 percent of employed civilians work
for non-profits and over 15 percent are state government workers (Table 14). The percentage of non-
profit and state government workers exceeds percentages for the same period in referent geographies.
In 2018, the percentage of the population that was self-employed was largely consistent with county and
state trends.

Table 14: Civilian Labor Force Classes in 2018




Source: U.S. Census Bureau ACS 5-Year Estimates 2018 (S2408).


4.2 Unemployment and Poverty
Contrary to local, state, and national trends, the unemployment rate in Emigration Canyon rose between
2010 and 2018 (Table 15). The 2018 unemployment rate for the Township (4.2 percent) was slightly higher
than the rate for Salt Lake City during the same year (4.1 percent). Both municipalities experienced an
unemployment rate lower than the nation but above that of Salt Lake County and the State of Utah.

                              Table 15: Unemployment Rates 2010-2018




                    Source: U.S. Census Bureau ACS 5-Year Estimates 2010 and 2018 (S2301).


Table 16 shows poverty rates from 2010 to 2018 for Salt Lake City, the County, the State, and the Nation.
Poverty data for Emigration Canyon was not available in 2010. In 2018, the U.S. Census Bureau reported
that 13.1 percent of the Canyon’s population was living below the poverty line (Table 16). The poverty
rate in Emigration Canyon was less than in neighboring Salt Lake City, but it was higher than the County

                Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 20

                                                                                                      91
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


and State. Between 2010 and 2018, poverty rates fell for referenced geographies. It is unclear whether
the same pattern was followed in Emigration Canyon.

It is important to note that the U.S. Census Bureau measures poverty by comparing income to a poverty
threshold (or the minimum income a person would need to pay for basic needs). Reported poverty rates
in Emigration Canyon may be an overestimate if individuals are independently wealthy. These residents
would have little or no reported income, potentially leading to misleading Census measurements.

4.3 Industry and Occupation
Table 17 provides a summary of employment by NAICS Industry Sectors for the years 2010 and 2018. In
2018, the industry sector employing the most Emigration Canyon residents was ‘Arts, entertainment, &
recreation, accommodation & food services.’ This sector employed 368 residents, or approximately 38
percent of Emigration Canyon’s civilian labor force (Table 17). This same industry sector comprises only
21 percent of Salt Lake County’s employment.

Between 2010 and 2018, the greatest increases in employment for Emigration Canyon were seen in the
‘Transportation & warehousing, utilities’, ‘Retail Trade’, and ‘Other services, except public administration’
sectors (Table 17). During the same period, Salt Lake County also experienced large increases in
Arts/Entertainment and Other Services, but employment in ‘Retail Trade’ decreased. The greatest
decreases in Emigration Canyon employment by percentage were experienced for the ‘Public
administration’ and ‘Professional, scientific, & management, administrative & waste management
services’ industry sectors.

4.4 Commuting Patterns
Over 95 percent of Emigration Canyon’s working population were employed in Salt Lake County in 2018
(Table 19). Only 4.1 percent were employed in Utah in a county other than Salt Lake County, and only
eight people (0.8 percent) worked in a state other than Utah. This is largely consistent with county-wide
trends, which show 92.5 percent of the county’s population employed in Salt Lake County in 2018 (Table
19).

of the canyon. Table 20 shows how these residents arrive to their place of work. In 2018, 76.3 percent of
residents drove alone to their job (Table 20). This was an increase from the percentage of people driving


               Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 26

                                                                                                      97
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


alone in 2010. Emigration Canyon residents commuted by personal vehicle more than residents of Salt
Lake City and Salt Lake County, but the percentage of people that drove alone was consistent with state
trends. The percentage of people carpooling decreased between 2010 and 2018 (Table 20). However,
moderate increases in the percentage of people walking, biking, and working from home occurred. As the
population of Emigration Canyon continues to grow, a shift away from driving alone will be needed to
reduce pressures on Emigration Canyon Road, especially during popular commute times.

Table 21: Jobs and Housing Balance 2010 to 2018




Source: U.S. Census Bureau ACS 5-Year Estimates 2010 and 2018 (DP04)(S2401). Total Employees data is derived
from recent estimates by Esri Business Analyst.


Table 21 shows the employment-to-population ratio and employee-to-housing-unit ratio for Emigration
Canyon and Salt Lake County for the years 2010 and 2018. The employment-to-population ratio is
calculated by dividing the number of employed civilians residing in a geography by the working-age
population of that same geography. Between 2010 and 2018, this ratio fell for Emigration Canyon, while
it remained steady for Salt Lake County (Table 21). The decrease in the ratio tells us that between 2010
and 2018, the percentage of the working-age population participating in the civilian labor force fell.

The employee-to-housing-unit ratio is calculated by dividing the number of employees working in an area
by the number of occupied housing units in that same area. In the best scenarios, this ratio would fall
somewhere between 0.75 and 1.5, indicating a relative match between employees and housing units
(EnviroAtlas, 2014). The ratio for Emigration Canyon was 0.2 in 2018, indicating that there are fewer jobs
in the Canyon then there are housing units (2018). The ratio indicates that there are not enough jobs
available in the Metro Township to support all the households that live there – Emigration Canyon
residents must commute elsewhere for work. Salt Lake County had an employee-to-housing-unit ratio of
1.6 in 2018, slightly outside of the desired range (Table 21). This number reveals that there are more
people employed in Salt Lake County than there are occupied housing units. In other words, people are
commuting to jobs in Salt Lake County from their residences outside of the county. This ratio has
implications for air quality, commuting patterns and road infrastructure, and housing demand.




                Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 27

                                                                                                               98
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


5.0 Housing
5.1 Housing Tenure
Table 22 reveals the count of owner-occupied, renter-occupied, and vacant housing units for the years
2010 and 2018. Between 2010 and 2018, Emigration Canyon gained 120 housing units (Table 22). A large
increase in owner-occupied homes was experienced, while renter-occupied and vacant units decreased.
This is opposite from the trend that occurred in Salt Lake County; between 2010 and 2018, Salt Lake
County experienced a greater increase in renter-occupied housing units than it did in owner-occupied
units. Sixty-seven units were vacant in Emigration Canyon in 2018, for a vacancy rate of 8.96 percent,
higher than the County and Salt Lake City but lower than the State.

Table 22: Housing Tenure Comparisons, Count




Source: U.S. Census Bureau ACS 5-Year Estimates 2010 and 2018 (S2501).


Figures 9 and 10 reveal trends in housing tenure as a percentage. In 2010, the proportion of housing units
in Emigration Canyon that were owner-occupied was consistent with the percentage of owner-occupied
units in the County and State (Figure 9). However, renter-occupied units were still few by comparison and
the vacancy rate was twice that of the State and Salt Lake City. By 2018, the percentage of owner-occupied
units had greatly increased in the Canyon. The percentage of renter-occupied housing units was only a
third of the statewide percentage and was far smaller than the percentage in Salt Lake City and County
(Figure 10).

5.2 Characteristics of the Housing Stock
The vast majority, 99 percent, of housing units in Emigration Canyon were classified as single-detached
units in both 2010 and 2018 (Table 23). In 2010, all other housing units were classified as single-attached
units, such as duplexes and townhomes located on a single parcel. By 2018, those units had been lost or
re-classified as ‘2 apartment’ multifamily structures, distinguished from single-attached units by shared
meters, lack of roof to ground floor wall separation, stacking of units, or lack of separate heating facilities
(U.S. Census Bureau, 2020).

Table 23: Emigration Canyon’s Housing Types 2010 and 2018




Source: U.S. Census Bureau ACS 5-Year Estimates 2010 and 2018 (DP04)

Table 24 shows the size of households in Emigration Canyon in 2018. Most households, 39.8 percent, were
comprised of two people (Table 24). There was also a large portion of households that contained four or
more people (25.6 percent). The median household size for the Canyon in 2018 was 2.68, higher than Salt
Lake City but lower than the County and State (ACS 5-Year Estimates 2018).

Table 24: Household Sizes for Emigration Canyon, 2018




Source: U.S. Cenus Bureau ACS 5-Year Estimates 2018 (S2501)


Table 25 describes the condition of housing units for various geographies in 2010 and 2018. In 2018, none
of Emigration Canyon’s housing units lacked plumbing facilities, kitchen facilities, or telephone service. In
general, the percentage of housing units lacking any of these qualities was low in Salt Lake City, the
County, and the State, with lack of telephone service being the most frequently cited adverse condition
(Table 25).


                Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 31

                                                                                                          102
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


Most households (98.1 percent) do have access to internet at home; however, the rugged terrain of the
Canyon can make reliable connection a challenge, and much of the service is at low intensity (Esri Business
Analyst – Electronics & Internet, 2020).
Another indication of housing condition is the age of the structure. Homes built before 1980 may not
adhere to current building and seismic codes and may not be energy-efficient, lacking adequate insulation
and energy saving devices (Wiebe, 2016). Between 2010 and 2018, there was a large increase in the
percentage of homes built before 1980 in Emigration Canyon. In other geographies, the percentage of
older homes decreased between 2010 and 2018, likely due to demolition or destructive events (Table 25).
While the percentage of homes built before 1980 in Emigration Canyon is lower than in nearby Salt Lake
City, it is higher than in both the County and State. The median year built of Emigration Canyon housing
units was 1978 in 2018, compared to 1958 for Salt Lake City, 1981 for Salt Lake County, and 1987 for Utah
(ACS 5-Year Estimates 2018, B253035).

Table 25: Conditions of Occupied Housing Units 2010-2018




Source: U.S. Census Bureau ACS 5-Year Estimates 2010 and 2018 (DP04)


Table 26 shows a comparison of heating fuel sources for housing units in 2018. All of Emigration Canyon’s
housing units were heated by utility gas, bottled, tank or LP gas, or electricity during this time period (with
the exception of eight that use some other fuel not listed)(Table 26). Burning wood or coal to heat a home
can have significant impacts on indoor air quality as well as ambient outdoor air quality (Longrange
Transboundary Air Pollution Convention Task Force, 2015). While no homes in the Canyon use this fuel
source, there are homes in nearby Salt Lake City and Salt Lake County that do. In 2018, no homes in
Emigration Canyon were relying on solar energy for heat.

Table 26: Comparison of Heating Fuel for Housing Units, 2018

5.3 Housing Costs and Affordability
Housing costs in Emigration Canyon are higher than in any other of the referenced geographies. In 2018,
the median monthly gross rent for Emigration Canyon residents was $1,224, while the median monthly
owner costs were $2,488 (Table 27). Monthly owner costs in the Canyon are almost $1,000 more than in
Salt Lake County. However, owner-costs are trending downward in the Township. Between 2010 and
2018, the monthly owner cost for households with mortgages dropped 31.1 percent (Table 27). Over that
same period, there was very little change in the median home value. Salt Lake County and the State of
Utah saw rises of approximately four percent in monthly owner costs, and both the County and State saw
an increase in median home value of over 15 percent. With a median home value of $696,600 in 2018,
Emigration Canyon is far more affluent than its neighboring geographies, but trends between 2010 and
2018 could reveal that the Canyon is drawing a more socioeconomically diverse residency.

Table 27: Median Rental and House Cost Comparison 2010-2018




Source: U.S. Census Bureau ACS 5-Year Estimates 2010 and 2018 (S2506) (B25064)


Table 28 compares housing costs and household incomes to determine the percentage of households that
are cost burdened in Emigration Canyon. If a household spends less than twenty percent of its income on
housing, it is classified as ‘Not cost-burdened’. Households spending 20-29 percent of their income on
housing costs are shown to be at ‘Cost-burden risk’. Finally, ‘Cost-burdened’ households are those that
spend more than thirty percent of their income on housing. Cost-burdened households may be at extreme
risk of losing their housing. When more than thirty percent of a household’s income is spent on housing,
it can be difficult to pay for other daily needs such as food and transportation. More so, households may


               Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 33

                                                                                                     104
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


fall behind on rent or mortgages if an unanticipated medical expense arises, costs incur to memorialize a
family member who has passed away, or a working member of the household suddenly loses a job. High
percentages of cost-burdened households reveal a lack of affordable housing in a market.

Table 28: Emigration Canyon Cost-Burdened Households by Income Range, 2017.




Source: U.S. Census Bureau ACS 5-Year Estimates 2017 (S2503); percentages may not sum to 100 percent with
rounding.


Table 28 reveals that there are households at every income level in Emigration Canyon that are cost-
burdened. In the lowest income ranges (household income below $35,000), 100 percent of households
are cost burdened (Table 28). In other words, sixty of the Canyon’s households are at extreme risk of
losing access to housing. But even at the highest income bracket ($75,000 or more), 2.8 percent of
households are cost-burdened. The percentage of households who are not cost-burdened is very low –
less than fifty percent for all income brackets except for those making $75,000 or more (Table 28). Salt
Lake County and Utah also have cost-burdened households at every income level, but the percentage of
households who are cost-burdened does not exceed sixteen percent at any income level in Utah or the
County (ACS 5-Year Estimates 2017). Unaffordable housing may force households that have established
their home in the Canyon to have to move elsewhere.

Table 29: Year Household Moved into Home, 2018 Data




Source: U.S. Census Bureau ACS 5-Year Estimates 2018 (DP04)


As further evidence of the affordable housing challenges associated with Emigration Canyon, Table 29 is
included to show the length of time that residents have lived in their home in Emigration County and
referenced geographies. In 2018, only 7.6 percent of households had moved into their home less than
four years prior (Table 29). The vast majority, 70.6 percent, of households had moved into their home


                Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 34

                                                                                                            105
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


more than ten years ago (2009 and prior). Significantly, 12.9 percent of households had been living in their
current homes for 30 years or more (Table 29). This is higher than in any other referenced geography.


6.0 Public Infrastructure and Facilities
6.1 Sanitary System
Waste and recycling are picked up by Wasatch Front Waste and Recycling on Fridays, at a cost of $204 per
year per household including one garbage can and one recycling bin (Wasatch Front Waste & Recycling
District, 2020). Vouchers for fee waivers for residents who haul their trash to the landfill themselves can
be obtained from Emigration Canyon’s Community Council Representative. Wasatch Front Waste &
Recycling District allows additional cans, for a $204 annual fee, or a Green Waste composting can for a
$114 fee (ibid). Waste and recycling are taken to the Trans-Jordan landfill located off Bacchus Highway in
South Jordan City.

6.2 Water System
Water services are provided to some of the Canyon’s residents by the Emigration Improvement District
(“District”). The District’s system includes two reservoir tanks, with a total of 1.3 million gallons capacity,
along with four deep wells located in the Emigration Oaks development (Emigration Canyon Improvement
District, 2020). The District, along with Salt Lake City, has also provided several fire hydrants in the lower
part of the Canyon to aid in fire suppression. The monthly base fee for residents’ water usage is $15; an
additional disproportionate fee is added based on usage (ibid). Two impact fees are also charged to
residents on a monthly basis and are determined by a household’s location within the Canyon.

The District reports serving approximately 300 canyon residents (Emigration Canyon Improvement
District, 2020). The remaining residents rely on private wells and septic systems for their drinking water
and wastewater management. This has significant implications for the health of residents as well as the
Canyon ecosystem. Water quality tests in the Canyon have estimated that as many as 40 percent of private
wells could have coliform counts that exceed thresholds established for safe drinking water (ibid). Septic
systems sometimes malfunction and can leak into the groundwater without owners knowing; the
Emigration Canyon Township page offers several resources for Canyon residents to help promote safe and
functioning wastewater treatment systems (Emigration Township,2020)

6.3 Public Safety
Emigration Canyon receives service from the Unified Police Department and Unified Fire Authority of
Greater Salt Lake. Emigration Canyon is served by the Canyon Patrol Unit, which covers all aspects of law
enforcement and community-oriented policing duties (Unified Police Department of Greater Salt Lake,
2019). This unit receives special training to address the unique conditions of the Canyon. Crime statistics
from the Unified Police Department are provided in Table 30.




                Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 35

                                                                                                          106
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


Table 30: Index Crime Comparisons: 2010, 2014, and 2018




Source: Utah Department of Public Safety, Crime in Utah Reports 2010, 2014, & 2018. 13


Crime rates in jurisdictions served by the Unified Police of Greater Salt Lake, as well as crime rates for Salt
Lake County in total, have dropped from 2010 to 2018. In 2018, the index crime rate per 1,000 people was
31 for areas served by the Unified Police of Greater Salt Lake (Table 30). This includes other townships
and unincorporated areas outside of Emigration Canyon; however, individual crime rates are not
available. It is generally believed that Emigration Canyon follows similar trends as the larger area served
by the Unified Police. The number of murders/homicides per year has remained steady for the Unified
Police of Greater Salt Lake, while reports of rape increased from 2010 to 2018 (Table 30).

Unified Fire Authority Station #119 is located at 5025 Emigration Canyon Road (Unified Fire Authority of
Greater Salt Lake, 2020). In addition to fire suppression, the Authority helps with emergency response,
fire safety inspections, security at community events, and educational school programs and certification
courses.

7.0 Land Use and Zoning
Emigration Canyon Metro Township includes approximately 12,000 acres in total land area. Land in
Emigration Canyon is primarily devoted to open space/natural resources and residential uses (Figures 12
and 13).

Figure 12: Emigration Canyon Zoning Map




Source: Prepared by Greater Salt Lake MSD GIS.




                Prepared by Long Range Planning, Greater Salt Lake Municipal Services District 2020 | 37

                                                                                                    108
Emigration Canyon Metro Township: Technical Assessment


Table 31: Emigration Canyon Zoning Districts




Source: Emigration Canyon Municipal Code, Title 19 Zoning.


Table 31 shows the zoning designations currently in use in Emigration Canyon. Nearly all the land is zoned
for Forestry and Recreation (FR) of various lot sizes (Table 31). Over 10,000 acres (88.5 percent) of the
Township’s land is zoned for FR-20. This zone allows forestry, recreational, and single-family residential
development, but only if such development will not damage natural and scenic resources in the Canyon.
Minimum lot size is 20 acres in the FR-20 zone. Minimal land is zoned for commercial or high-density
residential use. With limited commercial amenities, residents must travel outside of the canyon to reach
most of their daily needs, including food, employment, and education.

There are 14 entities classified as businesses in the Canyon, the most well-known being Ruth’s Diner and
Emigration Brewing Company (Esri Business Analyst – Business Locator, 2020); these two businesses
occupy two of the three commercial properties available in the canyon. The third commercially zoned
property in the Canyon has been placed under an easement so it is not developable. The only civic use is
the Fire Station located on Emigration Canyon Road. Two informal preserves exist in the Canyon: Perkins
Flat Preserve and Owl’s Meadow Preserve.

I.      INTRODUCTION
Emigration Canyon, located 10 minutes from the Salt Lake City downtown
metro center, is a unique environment with a mild grade, beautiful
surroundings, a residential and commercial community, and accessibility to
downtown. This unique context attracts various users—cyclists of differing
skill levels; commuting motorists who are familiar with the corridor; school
buses; visitors who are unfamiliar with the corridor; and pedestrians, runners,
and in-line skaters. This variety of motorists and active transportation users
results in myriad opinions on how the corridor should be used, what
problems or needs exist, and how best to address such issues.

In February 2014, Salt Lake County formed the Emigration Canyon Roadway
Improvement Committee (ECRIC), a group whose fundamental purpose was
to assess bicyclist- and motorist-related concerns in Emigration Canyon
through a facilitated collaborative process. Safety within a mixed-use corridor
was the overarching consideration. The result of this committee was the
development of recommendations for immediate action, and 79 specific
roadway improvements.

Salt Lake County provided funding to evaluate engineering deficiencies
throughout the canyon, develop costs for solutions recommended by the
ECRIC, and additional engineering analysis.

This report provides a summary of these findings.




Emigration Canyon Transportation Study                                             |2
                                                                                  116
II.     EXECUTIVE SUMMARY
Existing conditions of Emigration Canyon were identified in a collaborative
effort with input from the ECRIC, engineers, and the public. Nearly 200
comments were identified, as shown in Appendix A (separated by ECRIC,
engineering, and public). The comments received were similar in nature, and
were summarized into the following categories:
     • Geometric deficiencies
     • Traffic issues
     • Environmental concerns

Based on input from the ECRIC and the public, the need to address
geometric concerns had the highest rating of 60 percent, traffic concerns
rated at 30 percent, and environmental concerns rated at 10 percent. The
canyon was divided into 12 segments relative to geometric needs (narrow
shoulders or lane widths); a “hazard index” was then identified where higher
concentrations of needs were present on the corridor. See Appendix B.

Based on the needs identified, the project team identified solutions
throughout the canyon. Cost estimates were developed for solutions along
each of the 12 identified segments. The cost estimates were based on single
project solutions that coincided with the identified existing condition
deficiencies from the 200+ comments received. These segment estimates
identified $15 million in improvements throughout the canyon (see Figure 1).

Current Salt Lake County funding only allows for $1 million of potential
improvements to be completed during the 2016 construction season. These
solutions are summarized on Figure 2. Potential improvements for the $1
million projects have been progressed to 30% design plans (see Appendix C).
These improvements will be constructed by Salt Lake County over the 2016
construction season.

The County will continue to identify additional funding to construct future
improvements.

III. EXISTING CONDITIONS
In February 2014, Salt Lake County formed the Emigration Canyon Roadway
Improvement Committee (ECRIC), a group whose fundamental purpose was
to assess bicyclist- and motorist-related concerns in Emigration Canyon
through a facilitated collaborative process. The committee was composed of
residents, special-interest users, and a professional facilitator. The group
evaluated engineering, infrastructure, ecology, parking, enforcement, and
sanitation needs and concerns.


Crash Locations & Roadway Deficiencies

The project team collaborated with the ECRIC’s core group, provided
preliminary analyses, and conducted field reviews to confirm the ECRIC’s
findings and identify additional needs. Safety within a mixed-use corridor was
the overarching consideration. Traffic and safety analyses identified various
hot spots for crash locations (see Figure 3).

Extensive field reviews were completed to identify deficient roadway
conditions. These deficiencies include: limited sight distance, poor pavement,
slope instability, inadequate drainage, roadside hazards, tight turns, and
narrow shoulders. Over use of advance warning signs may be causing a lack
of respect for the advanced warning of bus stop and bicycles on the roadway.
In addition to the drainage concerns, instability of the side slopes, especially
after storm events, delivers rock debris on the shoulders. The debris forces
active transportation users to leave the shoulder and ride in the through
traffic lanes of Emigration Canyon. See Appendix D for a summary of the
identified roadway deficiencies.
